Barclays to cut about 5,000 jobs new
4 sources · 3 independent origins: BBC, Barclays, The Guardian

Barclays is cutting about 5,000 jobs, roughly 5% of its global workforce, as part of cost-saving plans that its bosses link to advances in AI. Barclays says 1,000 posts have already been closed and it expects a further 4,000 reductions to be complete by the end of this year. Venkatakrishnan also said AI "may have a more important role to play in the future".

Chief financial officer Anna Cross said about 60% of the group actions relate to support functions, including fewer developers, and the Guardian reports the cuts are part of plans to save more than £1.1bn in 2027. The BBC reports that the cost reductions are focused on the UK retail bank, but that a significant proportion of the cuts are expected to fall in India, where some UK back office functions are done.

Barclays reported £2.3bn in pre-tax profit for the July to September quarter, which the Guardian says is up 12% from £2.1bn a year earlier, while the BBC says it was slightly less than forecast.

Unite general secretary Sharon Graham said the company is using AI as an excuse to discard its workforce and called its approach short-term and kneejerk. Analyst Russ Mould of AJ Bell said Barclays was "reaching for the AI lever" but had failed to match expectations with its financial results, while Will Howlett of Quilter Cheviot said the cuts show how AI and automation could reshape banking.

Brent crude surges above $116-117 new
5 sources · 2 independent origins: The Guardian, Yahoo · Official data: WTI crude oil

Brent crude rose about 3.5% on Friday to pass $117 a barrel at one point, its highest level since April, according to The Guardian. Yahoo Finance reports that December Brent futures settled at $116.72 a barrel, the third consecutive weekly gain, while West Texas Intermediate closed just above $103. The Guardian says the jump followed Tehran's statement that it would attack oil facilities, undersea cables, datacentres and AI facilities in the Gulf if a new US-Israeli offensive were launched against Iran.

Yahoo Finance says the New York Times reported this week that the US and Israel have reportedly drawn up plans for renewed attacks on Iran after the Nov. 3 elections. Yahoo Finance also reports that President Trump has said he will not attack Iran before the midterms and has suggested fuel prices will fall afterward. Richard Hunter of Interactive Investor said reports that the US is considering renewed strikes after the midterms were enough for the price to pop, in a conflict now in its eighth month.

Susannah Streeter of Wealth Club said fears were rising of strikes on Iran's energy infrastructure after the 3 November elections, and that Ukraine's bombardment of Russian diesel refineries was also adding to supply concerns. Attacks in the region added to supply worries: Iran's Revolutionary Guards claimed to have struck four commercial ships in the Strait of Hormuz on Wednesday, and attacks hit Saudi airports and an Aramco oil field, with the US ordering some diplomats and nonessential personnel out of Saudi Arabia. Yahoo Finance adds that Saudi Aramco on Thursday ordered the evacuation of its Dhahran headquarters, its first such evacuation since the Iran war began.

Gulf Oil's Tom Kloza told Yahoo Finance that people should expect a lot of $5 gas prices in places like Michigan, Ohio, Minnesota, Illinois, and Indiana; Yahoo Finance reports US gasoline at $4.29 a gallon and diesel around $6.31.

US eases tariffs on Canadian goods new
3 sources · 2 independent origins: BBC, White House

The BBC says the US imposed a 50% levy on $20bn of Canadian goods after trade talks collapsed in August, and Canada responded with countermeasures. The BBC reports that the changes start on 30 October, and that the White House will also introduce tariffs on other items such as carpets and "waste products". The White House says the action reflects Trump's "continued efforts to defend American workers and ensure fair trade" and offsets Canada's "discriminatory measures".

Collins announced that the exemptions cover nine agricultural products, including blueberries, potatoes, apples and seaweed, while the White House also named processing equipment such as plywood sheets, packing boxes, paper products and certain machinery. Collins said she had repeatedly asked senior administration officials for relief and will keep seeking additional relief and a broader trade agreement with Canada.

Canada's internal trade minister Dominic LeBlanc said Canada is assessing the steps and "stands ready to work constructively and in good faith", while Prime Minister Mark Carney said Canada could sign a deal immediately if the US were ready but is willing to wait months or longer.

Procter & Gamble fiscal Q1 earnings new
3 sources · 2 independent origins: CNBC, Procter & Gamble

Procter & Gamble reported fiscal first-quarter results that beat Wall Street estimates, with core EPS of $2.00 against a $1.89 consensus and revenue of $23.3 billion against $22.82 billion expected. Shares rose about 6% on the day, which Yahoo Finance reported was to $166.19 in morning trading, while the SPDR S&P 500 ETF Trust (SPY) was down 1%.

Growth was driven mostly by volume: TIKR reports organic volume up 2.4% with price adding just 0.4%, and TIKR says Fabric & Home Care volume rose 4.3%. CNBC also reports that fabric and home care volume rose 4.3%. However, not every division grew: CNBC reports grooming volume fell 0.4% and healthcare volume fell 2.9%.

P&G kept its fiscal 2027 core EPS guidance of $6.89 to $7.11, and CNBC reports it raised its all-in sales growth outlook to 2% to 4% from 1% to 3%, a change it attributes to the Thorne acquisition. CEO Shailesh Jejurikar said the "strong start keeps us on track to deliver within our guidance ranges."

Costs are the main concern: core gross margin fell 150 basis points, and P&G now expects about $1.4 billion in after-tax commodity costs this fiscal year, up from about $1 billion. CFO Andre Schulten described a split consumer, saying on CNBC that high-income consumers trade up while low-income consumers stretch brands like Tide and Pampers to fit tight budgets. TIKR's author says volume-led growth is what one wants from a defensive stock but wants to see margins steady before expecting more upside, while 24/7 Wall St. on Yahoo Finance sees pricing power facing a test with lower-income shoppers.

Barclays Q3 profit new
2 sources · 2 independent origins: BBC, The Guardian

Barclays is cutting about 5,000 jobs, roughly 5% of its global workforce, as part of cost-saving plans that its executives link to advances in AI. Barclays says 1,000 posts have already been closed and it expects a further 4,000 reductions to be complete by the end of this year. Chief financial officer Anna Cross said around 60% of the group actions relate to support functions, including fewer developers, and the BBC reports that the cost reductions are focused on its UK retail bank, with a significant proportion of the job cuts expected to fall in India.

Chief executive CS Venkatakrishnan said the bank's deep technology investment and process simplification are driving the bulk of the efficiency gains so far, and said AI is the "icing on the cake not the cake". Barclays expects the efficiency measures to deliver savings of £1.1bn in 2027.

Barclays reported pre-tax profit of £2.3bn for July to September, up 12% from £2.1bn a year earlier, though the BBC says this was slightly less than forecast. The bank also announced a further £500m for investors, bringing payouts to shareholders since January to £2.8bn, and net interest income at its UK retail banking operations rose 6% to £2bn.

Unite general secretary Sharon Graham said Barclays is using AI as an excuse to discard its workforce and is taking a short-term approach. Russ Mould of AJ Bell said Barclays was reaching for the AI lever but had failed to match expectations with its results, while Will Howlett of Quilter Cheviot said the cuts show how AI and automation could reshape banking.

Moody's decision on France rating new
2 sources · 2 independent origins: Investing.com, Moody's

European stocks had their steepest weekly loss since early September, with the pan-European STOXX 600 falling 0.31% to 635.77 points on Friday, as tensions in France and higher oil prices dampened optimism from strong earnings, according to Reuters. The benchmark was down about 1.4% for the week, and the report says higher crude prices and the unrest in France have left investors jittery despite improved earnings estimates for European companies.

Investors were due to scrutinise Moody's decision on France's credit rating later on Friday, but the passage does not report the outcome.

Reuters says the turmoil has provided an explosive backdrop for the presidential election scheduled for April next year. Josh Stevens, chief investment officer at CresAlta Investment Management, says France will remain a market factor through the election cycle and that even if protests dissipate, the financial reality France faces does not change.

US initial jobless claims fall to 194K new
3 sources · single origin, unclear which · Official data: Initial jobless claims (weekly), Nonfarm payrolls (monthly change), Unemployment rate

US initial jobless claims slipped to a seasonally adjusted 194,000 for the week ended Oct. 17, the Labor Department reported Thursday. CNBC says the level is just off the 189,000 reported for the week of July 18, which was the lowest since September 1969. CNBC says claims have averaged 212,000 over the past year and the four-week moving average is down to 197,750. Bespoke reported that continuing claims increased to 1.728 million.

CNBC says the low claims come against an uncertain labor backdrop: payrolls rose just 29,000 in September, averaging 69,000 a month in 2026, and the unemployment rate rose to 4.2%. CNBC says layoffs have remained modest, with the layoff rate falling to 1% of the workforce over the prior 12 months. Thomas Simons, chief U.S. economist at Jefferies, said the claims data suggests labor market conditions are stable despite pessimistic consumer surveys, job-cut headlines and AI worries.

CNBC says the Fed meets next week, and markets expect the Fed to hold off on a rate hike in October but then to institute another quarter percentage point increase in December.

SpaceX competition hits telecom stocks new
2 sources · single origin, unclear which

Wall Street's main indexes opened lower on Friday, October 23, 2026, as caution prevailed after a sharp selloff in AI-linked stocks, while telecom stocks tumbled on fears that intensified competition from SpaceX could erode their market share. At the open, the Dow Jones Industrial Average fell 171.3 points, or 0.32%, to 52,696.7, the S&P 500 fell 26.2 points, or 0.34%, to 7,637.18, and the Nasdaq Composite dropped 114.9 points, or 0.44%, to 25,893.953.

In the same table, SpaceX's ticker SPCX was up 1.70% at 147.21 on volume of 25.66M, while tower companies Crown Castle (CCI), SBA Communications (SBAC) and American Tower (AMT) were up 11.16%, 6.48% and 5.95% respectively.

Bombardier $270 million US investment new
2 sources · single origin, unclear which

President Donald Trump said on Thursday, October 22, that Canadian jet maker Bombardier is investing $270 million in the United States. Trump said in a Truth Social post that the investment will create thousands of American jobs in Kansas, Texas, and Indiana. Bombardier did not respond to a request for comment on the investment details Trump announced, so the figure is so far Trump's claim rather than a company confirmation.

The announcement came days after Trump dropped threats to ban the company from the country. Bombardier had announced a $50 million investment in Wichita, Kansas the previous week.

L'Oreal Q3 sales new
2 sources · single origin (L'Oréal)

Chief executive Nicolas Hieronimus said, "We delivered an exceptional third quarter," and that L'Oréal significantly outperformed a beauty market estimated to have grown about 5 percent. For the first nine months, sales were 35.34 billion euros, up 7.7 percent reported and 6.9 percent like-for-like. Vara Consensus expected growth of 5.4 percent and Barclays forecast 5.7 percent. The BBC cites 7.7% growth for the quarter while WWD gives 7.2% like-for-like (7.7% adjusted) and 11.9% reported, so the sources use different measures.

WWD says growth was spurred by the Professional Products and Dermatological Beauty divisions and a strong recovery in Luxe, with Professional Products reported sales up 19.1 percent.

Hieronimus said China's market slowed down a tiny bit in Q3 but L'Oréal maintained its growth rate. WWD reports that travel retail returned to positive growth in the third quarter. Hieronimus said the Chinese airport stock issues were probably something that wouldn't be seen in Q4. For the rest of the year, Hieronimus said he expects the beauty market to grow around 5 percent and L'Oréal to keep outperforming at a slightly more moderate rate.

The BBC reports Hieronimus told investors that GLP-1 weight loss drug use is "having an impact on beauty consumption and skin care consumption in particular". Hieronimus said SkinCeuticals and Kiehl's have products designed to complement GLP-1 and its skin-sagging side-effects, and L'Oréal raised its stake in Galderma to 20% last December.

Bloom Energy Q3 earnings new
2 sources · single origin, unclear which

The decline followed an 8.7% drop on Oct 22, and the stock is down about 30% over one month.

Q3 EPS was $0.94 against a $0.69 estimate, and revenue was $1.24B against $1.06B, up 138% year over year. Gross margin reached 34.5%, operating income was $286M, and management raised full-year guidance again. Investing.com notes the stock also fell 13% after the Q2 beat, and says beats are priced in and the stock keeps falling anyway.

Investing.com's technical read is bearish: daily gauges read Strong Sell, and the price is below all 12 moving averages, including the daily SMA 200 at $219.33. It gives an RSI of 34.95 and says oversold readings can mean a bounce but do not signal a reversal.

CoreWeave bond and stock selloff
5 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 3 new articles this week · 5 independent origins: Axios, CNBC, Financial Times, Junk Bond Investor, The Wall Street Journal

CNBC describes junk-rated CoreWeave as the poster child of AI debt worries, and BlackRock's Rick Rieder says his firm has been more cautious around data center supply that is not hyperscaler guaranteed or backed. CNBC reports CoreWeave has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, and its five-year credit default swap spread is nearly 1,081 basis points, a level generally read as a sign of credit distress. Its debt-to-equity ratio is 702%, up from 290% a year ago, and CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capex this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

A Junk Bond Investor piece says the bonds traded above par in June, then slid into the low 90s over the summer as five-year CDS nearly doubled to about 850 basis points. Janus Henderson's John Lloyd says the yield to maturity on CoreWeave's 9.75% bonds due 2031 is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since April issuance.

Lloyd calls the bond selloff "a little bit of overreacting" and says he sees no huge fundamental change that would warrant it, other than higher financing costs. Marc Rubinstein is more critical, arguing CoreWeave functions as a conduit to capital markets whose cost of capital is rising, and calling it in effect a non-bank financial.

Luke Kawa noted record five-day net purchases of CoreWeave by Robinhood retail investors after the drop.

8 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 4 new articles this week · 3 independent origins: Investing.com, SpaceX, Yahoo

The BBC reports Tesla is valued at roughly $1.5 trillion and SpaceX at $2 trillion, which it says would make a combination one of the largest mergers in history. The talk came alongside SpaceX's results: revenue of $14.6 billion, up 161% from a year earlier and above the LSEG consensus of $12.72 billion, with net income of $4.4 billion. Sources give different moves for the stocks: the BBC said SpaceX rose 8% after hours and Tesla 2%, Investing.com said SpaceX settled 7.8% higher and Tesla 3.4%, and CNBC said SpaceX jumped 10% on Wednesday.

On Tesla's own earnings call on Wednesday, Musk said, "With regard to a potential merger with SpaceX, I think I said everything that should be said yesterday." Musk cautioned against treating a deal as certain, saying that doing so would be "overstating it" and that Tesla would need approval from at least 50% of its shareholders, which he called "quite a high hurdle to achieve". Musk said the committees consist of independent directors, that he is excluded from them as a "conflicted party", and that there is "no guarantee" they will reach agreement.

Futurum Group's David Nicholson said the merger is "100%. It's just a question of when," and Telos Wealth Advisors' Dan Sheehan said Musk will likely get his way within a few years. Sheehan also warned that Tesla shareholders would likely hold a "smaller slice of a much larger and more complex business" and that there is "no real precedent" for how Tesla would be priced in a deal.

China Q3 GDP beats forecasts
5 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 1 new article this week · 2 independent origins: BBC, National Bureau of Statistics

China's economy grew 4.5% in the third quarter of 2026, up from the previous quarter, according to official Chinese data reported by the BBC. The BBC says the pickup rebounded from a three-year low and was helped by an AI boom that boosted exports. China's National Bureau of Statistics said on Monday that the economy "sustained steady development against pressure" and showed "momentum with new growth drivers and improving structure". Official figures showed high-tech goods sales up 14.3% and battery and industrial robot production up more than 29%, while property investment fell by more than 20% in the first nine months.

The BBC says 4.5% sits at the bottom end of Beijing's 4.5%-5% annual target, which analysts have said gives policymakers room to address economic challenges. However, the sources give different Q3 growth figures: the BBC reports 4.5%, while The Diplomat says the economy expanded 4.4% and was below the government's target range. Bespoke's 1.2% is a quarter-on-quarter rate, while the other figures are presented as growth against a target, so the passages do not make clear whether they measure the same thing.

Oxford Economics researcher Sheana Yue said manufacturing and high-tech exports have boosted growth despite weak investment and spending, and that there are still no signs the property slump will end soon. Dave Chia, an economist at Moody's Analytics, said the effects of Beijing's targeted support measures have yet to be seen and that reliance on a "single external engine" in exports is a risk. The Diplomat says that the prospect of tighter controls over AI exports could create fresh pressures for an economy that is already facing severe challenges.

French 10-year yield hits 5%
4 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 3 new articles this week · 2 independent origins: Investing.com, The Guardian · Official data: 10-year Treasury yield

The South China Morning Post reported that yields topped 5% for a third consecutive day on Friday. The Guardian reported that the yield rose more than 8 basis points to 5% in morning trading before easing to 4.99%. On Thursday it rose a further nine basis points to 5.089% in early trading, its highest since July 2022, and moved above the US Treasury 10-year yield for the first time since 2013, according to The Guardian. Sources differ on how far back the high goes: the BBC's Briefing Room described a "26 year high", while Reuters and The Guardian said 24 years and the first time since 2002.

Reuters said the premium over German Bunds was edging back toward early October's 15-year high of nearly 160 basis points, and The Guardian said the spread had risen to its highest level in almost 15 years. Deutsche Bank strategist Jim Reid said the sell-off was spreading, with the Italian spread over German bonds widening 3.8 basis points to 116 and Spain's widening 3.1 basis points to 66.

The Guardian says France's 2025 deficit was 5.1% of GDP and its debt 115.6% of GDP, and that gridlock in parliament has blocked President Emmanuel Macron's plan to raise the pension age from 62 to 64. Matthew Ryan of Ebury said the political risk premium in France looked unlikely to fade soon and that it was unclear whether the 2027 budget would clear parliament, adding that any budget might be too full of concessions to address the deficit. John Hardy of Saxo said the French debt situation "isn't tractable" and that France needs to make proper fiscal adjustments.

Carsten Brzeski of ING said rising yields had reinforced the European Central Bank's "restrictive" stance, that the ECB is very likely to stay on hold next week and delay any next rate hike decision to December, and that without any solution to France's fiscal woes bond markets have taken over the ECB's role, making rate hikes less likely. Reuters reported that Carrefour fell 13% after cutting its guidance due to the recent protests, and that the euro was weighed down by anti-government protests in France and a worsening fiscal outlook.

Judge orders Meta to pay $25bn over Facebook data misrepresentations
3 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 1 new article this week · 2 independent origins: BBC, The Guardian

A New Mexico judge ordered Meta to pay $25bn on Wednesday after a trial found the company had spent years lying to the public about how it handled Facebook user data. The BBC reports this is the largest fine ever against Meta, and the case stemmed from the Cambridge Analytica scandal.

A jury found Meta violated state laws more than 40 million times, and Judge Francis Mathew said the violations were "willful" and spanned more than 11 years, from 2010 to 2021. Mathew wrote that communications between senior executives, including Mark Zuckerberg, were "designed to mislead the public."

The full penalty, based on thousands of violations, was calculated at $219bn, but Mathew reduced it to $25bn. New Mexico's lawyers sought up to $40bn, while Meta argued anything beyond $3.4bn would be unconstitutional.

New Mexico Attorney General Raúl Torrez said the result shows that "no corporation is too powerful to be held accountable." A Meta spokesperson did not immediately respond to the BBC's request for comment.

Reuters, via Investing.com, described the penalty as one of several mounting legal challenges, and noted that Meta reported better-than-expected quarterly revenue of $64.60 billion the same day.

IBM reported third-quarter 2026 results on October 21, beating Wall Street forecasts, with revenue of $17.1 billion and adjusted earnings per share of $2.95. Krishna framed this as an opening for IBM, saying "That is the tailwind for us."

Fed expected to hold, with hikes priced for late 2026 and early 2027
5 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 5 new articles this week · single origin (CNBC) · Official data: CPI inflation (year over year)

The Federal Open Market Committee is widely expected to leave rates unchanged at its 27–28 October meeting, with analysts and futures pricing pointing to hikes later, in December and March 2027. PIMCO says markets are pricing roughly an 80% chance of a hold in October. The Daily Economy article says the Atlanta Fed's tracker gives roughly a 94 percent chance of at least one more hike by year-end, and CME FedWatch shows an 80 percent probability of a December quarter-point increase.

PIMCO expects the FOMC to hold at 3.75%–4.0% while keeping a clear tightening bias, with a 25-basis-point hike in December the most likely next move. Employ America's base case is a hold next week followed by hikes in December and March, and TD Securities likewise expects a hold, then a resumption of tightening in December and March. TD Securities argues the December hike is "all but locked in" barring a shock, but warns that one notably bad inflation report or strong labor data could lead markets to price a 50bp increase.

The Daily Economy reports September CPI rose 3.7% over the previous year, up from 3.4% in August, and that August core PCE was 3.0%, with the September CPI data pushing the inflation side of the Taylor rule toward higher rates.

Officials differ on timing: New York Fed President John Williams has said the Fed has time to gather more information, while the Daily Economy says more hawkish officials, like Hammack and Dallas Fed President Lorie Logan, have argued for tighter policy immediately. PIMCO says a few participants may dissent from a hold, but argues the Fed has room for a measured approach, pausing to see how tightening already priced in is affecting the economy.

TD Securities strategists say FX markets remain meaningfully long the dollar going into the FOMC, and that data suggests the Fed has the highest rate hike pressures in the G10.

4 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 1 new article this week · single origin (Office for National Statistics)

UK annual inflation rose to 3.5% in the 12 months to September, up from 3.1% in August, driven mainly by sharply higher petrol and diesel prices, according to the Office for National Statistics. CNBC reports that the figure was hotter than the 3.4% economists polled by Reuters had forecast, and that it was the highest annual rate in almost a year.

Markets are pricing in a 98% chance that the Bank of England raises rates by 25 basis points to 4% next month, according to LSEG data cited by CNBC. Scott Gardner of J.P. Morgan Personal Investing says accelerating services inflation raises concern about second-round effects beyond energy and strengthens the case for a hike, and Suren Thiru of the ICAEW likewise says a November rate rise is falling into place.

Healey says he will keep "fiscal discipline" while giving businesses and families "breathing space", whereas Suren Thiru calls the Budget "a perilous high-wire act".

Federal Reserve raises rates in September
4 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 5 new articles this week · single origin (Federal Reserve) · Official data: CPI inflation (year over year)

The Federal Reserve raised interest rates by a quarter point to a range of 3.75%-4.00% on Sept. 16, its first hike in about three years, according to the sources. The Motley Fool's account says Fed Chair Kevin Warsh and 11 other FOMC members voted unanimously (12-0) for the increase. The BBC reports the Fed cited "too high" inflation, and that the move came despite fierce opposition from President Donald Trump, who had called for rates to be cut.

Sue Roesler of Ag Update writes that the board wanted to get ahead of "stuck" inflation above 3 percent, against a 2 percent target. US inflation was 3.7% in September, up from 3.4% in August, according to the BBC, with fuel costs a factor. Motley Fool writer Sean Williams says September producer prices rose 6.3% (headline) and 5% (core), and that Trump's tariffs and the ongoing Iran war are directly lifting consumer prices, while AI hardware companies' pricing power has increased prices for chips and memory used in tech devices.

A Motley Fool commentator argues more hikes are coming: he reads Warsh's repeated phrase "dose of accommodation" as suggesting the rate-hiking cycle is likely to ramp up in December and throughout 2027. Motley Fool articles say futures pricing implies three more quarter-point increases by mid-2027, and that CME's FedWatch tool shows a 72% probability of rates rising by at least 75 basis points by Sept. 15, 2027.

The Motley Fool writers differ on the market impact: one calls a rapid hiking cycle "terrible news" for the AI-driven bull market, while another says history shows no single market reaction to hikes and recommends buy-and-hold.

SpaceX quarterly results
6 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 3 independent origins: CNBC, Investing.com, SpaceX

SpaceX reported third-quarter revenue of $14.6 billion, up 161% from a year earlier and above the LSEG consensus estimate of $12.72 billion, and Elon Musk said on the earnings call that he is considering merging SpaceX with Tesla. Musk said special committees at both companies have been formed to examine the idea, but that they have not agreed terms and there is no guarantee they will. Musk said he is a "conflicted party" and so is not part of the committees, and that at least 50% of Tesla's shareholders would need to approve a deal, which he called "quite a high hurdle to achieve". Musk cautioned that assuming a merger is certain would be overstating it, while saying he thinks there is "a lot of merit to combining the companies".

Net income was $4.4 billion and operating income $2.8 billion, compared with a net loss of $1.05 billion a year earlier, according to CNBC. The AI business, which SpaceX calls Super Intelligence, was the largest unit, with revenue of $7.56 billion versus $813 million a year earlier, while Starlink revenue rose to $5.2 billion from $3 billion. BBC reported that over the first nine months SpaceX as a whole was not profitable, losing $1.1 billion, and Investing.com said capital expenditures climbed to $19.6 billion. SpaceX forecast first-quarter 2027 revenue of $33 billion to $37 billion, against an average analyst estimate of $22.2 billion, according to LSEG.

Sources differ on the after-hours reaction: the Guardian said SpaceX shares rose 2%, while the BBC said 8%. SpaceX stock settled 7.8% higher on Wednesday according to Investing.com, and CNBC reported a 10% jump. Marc Dizard of Huntington Bank said SpaceX had "crushed" expectations and that the market reaction reaffirmed that the AI trade is still intact. Truist Securities analysts said in a Tuesday note that commentary on computing during the earnings call supported "continued pricing power and shorter payback periods" for AI cloud providers, and that SpaceX's quarter was the first data point of earnings season to support the bull case for AI cloud providers, explaining that demand appears "insatiable".

Questions about Starshield and Ukraine overshadowed the results: the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk personally ordered the throttling, while Musk denied responsibility and said the US government controlled Starshield.

UK Autumn Budget ahead of 28 October
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · 2 independent origins: BBC, Office for National Statistics

The ONS said the government borrowed £24.5bn in September, £1.8bn more than a year earlier, leaving borrowing this financial year £6bn above the OBR's forecast, while national debt topped £3tn for the first time. The BBC reports that debt as a share of economic output, the measure used for the fiscal rules, fell, and that the OBR's March estimate of £23.6bn headroom is expected to have shrunk, with KPMG believing it could have fallen to £12bn and the Institute for Fiscal Studies estimating it could be £20bn.

CNBC says the 3.5% figure was hotter than the 3.4% forecast by economists polled by Reuters, and the ONS said motor fuel prices rose 29.8% in the year, with petrol at its highest since August 2022. The BBC links the fuel price rises to the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz by Iran after US and Israeli attacks, and ONS chief economist Grant Fitzner said airfares, computer games and live events also contributed.

Healey says the Budget will stick to the fiscal rules while driving growth and giving businesses and families "breathing space", and Labour has committed not to raise income tax, National Insurance or VAT. The BBC notes he has refused to rule out other tax increases, and bank bosses told the BBC they expect a tax rise on the sector.

Suren Thiru of the Institute of Chartered Accountants in England and Wales called the Budget "a perilous high-wire act for the Chancellor", and Ruth Gregory of Capital Economics expects a cautious Budget that leaves the toughest fiscal choices for next year.

The Conservatives said the figures showed Labour had "lost control of the economy", and Shadow Chancellor Andrew Griffith called for Healey to use his Budget to tackle the deficit; markets price a 98% chance of a Bank of England rate rise to 4% next month, according to LSEG data cited by CNBC.

UK national debt and September borrowing
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (Office for National Statistics)

The UK's national debt topped £3 trillion for the first time in official figures published on Wednesday 21 October 2026, a week before Chancellor John Healey's first Budget. Despite the headline total, debt as a share of economic output, the measure used to judge the fiscal rules, fell.

The Office for National Statistics said the government borrowed £24.5bn in September, £1.8bn more than a year earlier, as higher inflation helped push up debt interest costs. The cost of servicing the debt has reportedly risen sharply over the past year, and CNBC says the economy is struggling to grow. The borrowing figures arrived alongside inflation data showing UK inflation rose to 3.5% in the year to September from 3.1% in August, which the BBC and CNBC link mainly to fuel prices.

Healey said he would maintain "fiscal discipline" in the Budget on 28 October while driving growth and giving families and businesses "some breathing space". The Conservatives' Shadow Chancellor Andrew Griffith said the figures showed Labour had "lost control of the economy" and urged Healey to tackle the deficit.

Ruth Gregory of Capital Economics said the borrowing figures "painted a dismal picture" and were higher than analysts and the OBR had expected, and she expects a cautious Budget that leaves the toughest fiscal choices for next year. Suren Thiru of the Institute of Chartered Accountants in England and Wales said rising inflation makes the Budget "a perilous high-wire act for the Chancellor" by shrinking fiscal headroom.

September US housing starts decline
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (CNBC)

US housing starts fell 5.2% in September, a larger drop than the 0.4% decline expected, according to Commerce Department data reported by CNBC. Bespoke reported the annualized level at 1.221 million against forecasts of 1.305 million. Building permits fell 0.2% against a Dow Jones consensus for a 2% gain, and single-family permits totaled 845,000, down 4% from August and the lowest since 2023, according to CNBC. Bespoke, by contrast, described building permits as right in line with expectations, so the two sources differ on permits.

Separately, the National Association of Realtors reported that pending home sales fell 7.3% in September to an all-time low in records back to 2001, against an expected 0.4% decline. Lawrence Yun, the NAR's chief economist, says market conditions weakened because of fast-rising mortgage rates, which CNBC puts at 7.43%. Yun adds that mortgage rates could be topping out, though CNBC notes futures imply a strong chance of Fed hikes in December and March 2027.

Goldman Sachs economist Ronnie Walker expects affordability headwinds to increasingly dominate in 2027, weighing further on activity. Bespoke says the housing industry continues to struggle under high interest rates, with homebuilder ETF XHB making new 52-week lows.

Eurozone inflation 3.8% in September
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (European Central Bank)

Eurozone headline inflation rose to 3.8% in September, up from 3.2% in August, according to EU data cited by the BBC. The European Central Bank (ECB) says the rise mostly reflected a surge in energy inflation and, to a lesser extent, higher food and services inflation. The ECB says core inflation increased only slightly to 2.5%, that underlying measures remained broadly stable, and that wages have so far shown no material response to the energy shock.

The ECB expects higher energy prices to keep headline inflation well above target into the first half of 2027, with a return to around target towards the end of 2027. The ECB argues the energy shock is too large to "look through" but that a measured response remains appropriate to keep inflation in check, noting it sees no evidence at this stage of significant pass-through of energy prices to other prices and wages beyond what is already included in its projections.

The BBC reports the ECB has raised interest rates twice in recent months to 2.5%, while US inflation was 3.7% in September and UK inflation was 3.5%.

UK unemployment falls to 4.7% as vacancies drop
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (Office for National Statistics)

The UK's unemployment rate fell to 4.7% in the three months to August, the lowest since July last year, the Office for National Statistics (ONS) said. Youth unemployment for those aged 16 to 24 was 15.6% in June to August, down from 16.4% in March to May but above the 14.6% of a year earlier. Average earnings excluding bonuses rose 3.7% in the three months to August, up from 3.5% last month. Taking inflation into account, wages grew 0.8% in real terms.

Vacancies fell by 21,000 in the three months to September to 691,000, with the ONS saying small firms were citing labour costs as a key factor in scaling back hiring. ONS deputy director David Freeman said the labour market remained "broadly flat", with the largest fall in vacancies since the start of the year concentrated mainly in smaller businesses.

Work and Pensions Secretary Pat McFadden said the figures "show the resilience of our labour market", though he added that "too many young people still find themselves locked out of employment". Conservative shadow chancellor Andrew Griffith countered that "businesses can't afford to hire", blaming attacks on employers, taxes on jobs and red tape.

Ashley Webb of Capital Economics called the fall in unemployment "unexpected" and said it suggests the labour market "continues to recover", but he still expects hiring to stay subdued. Louise Murphy of the Resolution Foundation said the labour market has performed poorly in recent years but that there may be "green shoots". Bank of England policymaker Megan Greene, citing the fall in unemployment from 5.2% to 4.7%, argues that labour market slack may have peaked and that she believes there is less slack than expected.

The ONS's data quality has been criticised, and the ONS said response levels to the Labour Force Survey had improved but "some volatility" remained.

UK regular pay growth outpaces inflation
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (Office for National Statistics)

The latest official figures show UK regular pay growing ahead of inflation in the three months to August, according to the BBC. Average annual growth in pay excluding bonuses was 3.7% over that period, and after taking inflation into account regular pay grew by 0.8% between June and August.

Meanwhile, UK consumer price inflation rose to 3.5% in the year to September, the highest in nearly a year and above the Bank of England's 2% target, which the BBC says was slightly higher than economists had predicted and driven by higher petrol and diesel prices. Inflation is widely expected to rise again in the coming months, and the BBC links this to expected petrol price rises and a 4% increase in household energy bills from 1 October. On interest rates, the BBC reports that Bank governor Andrew Bailey said at the September meeting that energy price volatility made rate rises more likely, with the base rate currently 3.75%.

Greene says the divergence between the two measures has become unusually large by historical standards since early 2026. After adjusting for compositional effects and bonuses, Greene's figures show wage growth of 3.4% on AWE and 4.5% on RTI, and she argues both remain above levels consistent with a 2% inflation target and suggest there may be less labour market slack than previously thought. Greene concludes that wage disinflation appears to have stalled above levels consistent with 2% inflation, and says forward-looking indicators point to wage growth plateauing nearer 3.5% than 3% over the coming year, with risks skewed to the upside in her view.

UK approves development of Jackdaw gasfield in North Sea
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (BBC)

Chancellor John Healey announced an Energy Wealth Fund at the Jackdaw gas approval announcement on Wednesday, with £2bn of funding diverted from the existing National Wealth Fund. Healey said the proceeds of the oil and gas coming ashore now can be invested for future generations. He said that had this been done when North Sea oil was first discovered in the 1980s, Britain would now have a sovereign wealth fund worth probably a trillion pounds. The trillion-pound figure comes from internal Treasury modelling, which assumed the UK's historical North Sea receipts of £200bn had been invested with no withdrawals and grown at a rate comparable to Norway's.

The BBC's Faisal Islam notes Norway has amassed a $2.4tn fund from the same North Sea, and that Norway's fund only began in earnest in 1996. A Treasury insider told Islam the government wants to learn from Norway's principle rather than copy its model wholesale; the insider also called the failure to systematically invest the receipts a "missed opportunity".

Islam says the UK fund will differ from Norway's, which must invest abroad, because the UK plan is to invest domestically in green energy, and he says it may struggle to match Norway's performance. Islam adds that nobody expects the £2bn fund to be worth £2tn by 2056, and that UK energy tax revenues are forecast to dwindle as North Sea production slows, though Jackdaw and field extensions may ease the rate of slowing.

Santos Q3 results
2 sources · tracked since Oct 21 · single origin (Santos)

Santos narrowed its 2026 production and sales volume forecasts on Thursday after temporary disruptions at its Pikka oil project in Alaska, even though it reported record quarterly output and a 78% jump in third-quarter revenue. Santos now expects 2026 production of 99 to 102 million barrels of oil equivalent (mmboe), down from a previous range of 99 to 105 mmboe, and sales volumes of 102-105 mmboe, down from 102-108 mmboe. Santos said the narrower outlook reflected delays in commissioning a seawater treatment plant at Pikka, output curtailments following a weather event, and a shutdown of the Kuparuk pipeline after a road accident. Pikka briefly reached its minimum expected plateau production rate before quarter-end, and Santos said output would be held at roughly 70,000 barrels per day for a short period before ramping up toward 80,000 barrels per day.

Third-quarter sales revenue rose to $2.01 billion from $1.13 billion a year earlier, matching Visible Alpha estimates, while production climbed 23% to a record 26.3 mmboe. Santos said production benefited from Barossa reaching steady-state production and Pikka's continued ramp-up, and that revenue was boosted by higher LNG prices, higher LNG sales volumes and stronger crude and condensate sales. The company generated record free cash flow from operations of about $1.07 billion in the quarter, and more than 50% of it came from its international operations in Papua New Guinea and Alaska, according to Santos.

Chief Executive Kevin Gallagher said the "inflection point" expected from the start-up of Barossa and Pikka had now arrived. Santos shares fell more than 1% in early trading.

Key points
  • Trading costs fell from brokerage fees of 2% or more and 1/8th-dollar spreads (until 1997) to pennies; Vanguard's first index fund had an 8.5% sales load.
  • Regulation FD, imposed in 2000, ended selective disclosure of material information, making it harder for large active investors to win on private information.
  • Andrew Ang estimates federal taxes would have cut the 10%-plus long-term stock return to 7%, and his figures exclude state and local taxes.
  • Michael Milken acted on Hickman's 1900-1943 bond study, which showed higher yields more than offset higher default rates; almost no one else acted on it.
  • Human nature is the constant across market cycles, and more data does not make the future easier to predict, so guard against overconfidence.
Title check: Title implies markets always differ; text documents concrete changes in costs and rules, but stresses unchanging human nature.
How I Think About Investing in Bonds
1d ago · ~5 min read
Author explains buying bonds now that yields are high again; he prefers intermediate duration (5-6 years or less) and avoids long-term Treasuries' rate risk.
Key points
  • Bond returns tend to track starting yields, and expected bond returns are the highest since Apple launched its first iPhone.
  • Duration measures rate sensitivity: TLT's duration of around 15 years implies roughly a 15% price drop for a 1% rise in rates.
  • He avoids long-term bonds and prefers duration of 5-6 years or shorter, since he doesn't like timing interest rate moves.
  • He bought tax-efficient bonds in his brokerage account, citing a 5.5% yield, for a future goal, dry powder, or steady income.
  • Bonds diversify his still equity-heavy portfolio; he says he is not predicting a stock collapse or an interest rate collapse.

15 Year Stock Market Returns
5d ago · ~2 min read
Recent above-average S&P 500 returns fit a long good-and-bad cycle of unknown length; the author advises lowering return expectations for a safety margin.
Key points
  • Through end of September, S&P 500 annual returns were +15.7% over 15 years, +11.2% over 20 years, and +11.9% over 50 years, against a 100-year average of about 10%.
  • Returns over 15 years and shorter are above average, while 20 to 50 years are close to the long-term average.
  • Rolling 15-year returns over 100 years show waves that move from good returns to bad returns, though the timing of each cycle varies.
  • The author compares 1995-1999 (+37.2%, +22.7%, +33.1%, +28.3%, +20.9%) with 2019 through 2026 so far, but says the sample is small and nobody knows how long the current cycle lasts.
  • Lower return expectations give a bigger margin of safety: if bad things happen you are prepared, and if good things continue it is a nice surprise.

Brunch Reads – 10/18/26
5d ago · ~2 min read · paywalled: summary of the free preview only
Bespoke's linkfest recaps Fuller's 1933 Dymaxion car patent and a WSJ piece on Britain's surging sickness and disability benefit claims.
Key points
  • R. Buckminster Fuller filed a patent application for the Dymaxion car on this date in 1933; the patent was granted in December 1937.
  • The Dymaxion reportedly got around 30 miles per gallon, could carry up to 11 people, and only three were built.
  • A fatal crash involving the prototype hurt confidence in the project, funding ran out, and it never reached mass production.
  • Britain's working-age population receiving sickness or disability benefits has grown more than 60% since 2019, with mental-health claims accounting for much of the increase.
  • Annual spending on these benefits across all ages now exceeds £100 billion, and the government spends £25 on benefits for young people for every £1 it spends helping them find work.

      Barry Ritholtz 13 items this week
      Defending Jonathan Miller
      12h ago · ~4 min read
      Shaun Osher argues that brokerages hiding listings in private networks undermines the market data that Jonathan Miller's reports depend on, and that transparency helps consumers and agents.
      Key points
      • Miller's market reports depend on agent-shared data, and some of that data is increasingly disappearing.
      • Osher says brokerages are building private listing networks, holding inventory and controlling who sees it and when.
      • Osher distinguishes client-requested privacy, which is legitimate, from the industry withholding information for its own benefit.
      • Osher argues that tracking contract-signing data shows what is happening now, while closings show what happened months ago.
      • Osher urges the industry to give data analysts like Miller the full numbers rather than blaming them for unwelcome results.

      At The Money: Can Dividend Investing Give You Yield AND Growth?
      1d ago · ~18 min read
      Bahnsen argues dividend growth investing offers downturn defense and inflation protection, warns that high yields can be traps, and prefers cash-backed dividends to buybacks.
      Key points
      • Most dividend growth portfolios would have been up roughly "seven, 8%" a year from 2000 to 2009, while the S&P was up 0%.
      • From 2009 to 2026, the S&P compounded 15% a year; dividend growth beat the market in about half of those years.
      • A stock falling from $100 to $50 with a $4 dividend doubles its yield but halves the investor's value, so high yield can be a trap.
      • Dividends are cash and harder to fake than earnings; buybacks go first in trouble, and Bahnsen's portfolio had zero dividend cuts from the financial crisis through COVID.
      • Bahnsen holds 30 to 35 companies, sells when the thesis breaks, and trims names that have grown to roughly a 1% yield.

      The Long History of Underreporting Inflation
      4d ago · ~6 min read
      Author argues US inflation measurement changes since WWII consistently reduced reported inflation, culminating in 2025's CPI collection suspension.
      Key points
      • Mitchell Committee era: COLI understated price increases by 4 percentage points from 1941–44; BLS then renamed it the Consumer Price Index in 1945.
      • Switch to owners' equivalent rent in January 1983 kept the 2002–07 housing bubble out of CPI, per the author.
      • Boskin Commission (December 1996) reported an upward bias of 1.1 points a year; the author calls it political, aimed at cutting COLAs and bracket creep.
      • The Fed moved its preferred gauge to PCE in 2000 and core PCE in 2004, lowering the inflation number it is judged against by about 0.5 pts/yr versus CPI.
      • In 2025 BLS suspended CPI collection in several metro areas, and the shutdown meant no October 2025 CPI release at all.

      Prediction Markets Are AccurateTitle partly delivers
      16h ago · ~1 min read
      Study of about one million resolved forecasts on four platforms finds probabilities broadly well calibrated, with systematic over- or underestimation in some consequential areas.
      Key points
      • Analyzes about one million resolved contracts and questions from Polymarket, Kalshi, Manifold and Metaculus, spanning twelve domains.
      • Forecast probabilities were overall highly calibrated: events given p% occurred about p% of the time.
      • Calibration held across platforms and many domains, but fine-grained analysis showed systematic over- or underestimation in highly consequential areas.
      • Authors stress this does not mean certainty about individual events, but large-scale aggregation carries meaningful information about uncertainty.
      Title check: Title promises accuracy; text reports overall calibration, but notes systematic biases in consequential areas and no certainty for single events.
      Will bystander economies be able to finance their governments?
      22h ago · ~4 min read
      An AI-driven rise in real interest rates could leave non-adopting economies like Spain unable to stabilize debt, while adopters can tax capital income.
      Key points
      • In the benchmark scenario, the ten-year real yield jumps from 5.1% to 13.6% on impact, making government borrowing more expensive.
      • Bystander Spain, growing at 1.6%, would need a primary surplus of 11% of GDP in 2030, 26% in 2035, and 44% forever with unit elasticity.
      • On a balanced primary budget, the bystander's debt doubles by 2033 and triples by 2035, and the author says no tax system raises that surplus.
      • The US-style adopter could cover its surplus with a 36% tax on net capital income (unit elasticity) or 20% (strong complementarity across tasks).
      • The author calls the model a benchmark, not a forecast, and is somewhat more optimistic, citing spillovers from American and Chinese AI and possibly elastic capital supply.
      How Long Do Wealth Shocks Persist? Less Than Three Generations in England, 1700–2026
      1d ago · ~1 min read
      Using random family-size shocks in pre-1880 England, the paper finds biology-driven wealth shocks fade by three generations; long-run wealth persists via inherited behaviors, not transfers.
      Key points
      • Fertility in pre-1880 England was a biological lottery, so family-size shocks serve as a natural experiment for measuring wealth shock persistence.
      • For richer families, family size strongly influenced child wealth.
      • Biology-induced wealth shocks had no impact on descendant wealth by three generations later.
      • Wealth itself persisted across more than five generations, so long-run wealth mainly derives from sources other than inherited wealth.
      • Persistence reflects inherited behaviors tied to wealth accumulation and retention, not wealth transfers alone.
      The Causal Effect of Foreign Ideas on U.S. Innovation
      2d ago · ~1 min read
      Foreign technologies serve as inputs to U.S. innovation; migrant-settlement instruments show causal effects on U.S. invention and local jobs.
      Key points
      • 54% of U.S. technologies build on at least one foreign input, and the most fertile U.S. technologies rely on foreign inputs most heavily.
      • Using historical migrant settlement as exogenous variation, counties more exposed to a foreign country's knowledge absorb more of its technologies and invent more U.S. technologies building on them.
      • Foreign ideas shape the direction of innovation: counties are far more likely to pioneer technologies whose foreign inputs are already in their local idea stock.
      • Effects persist: decades later, counties that absorbed more of an origin's ideas host more jobs working with those ideas.
      • The study builds a technology genealogy from 13 million patents filed between 1887 and 2024.
      Saving for the machines
      3d ago · ~1 min read
      A task model where automation's growth boom depends on capital supply: fixed 23% saving yields 2.2% growth versus 11.7% under optimal saving.
      Key points
      • Growth depends on capital supply for boom size; task structure sets boom duration and wage evolution.
      • The same technology path gives 11.7% annual growth with optimal saving but only 2.2% with saving fixed at the calibrated U.S. 2025 level of 23%.
      • The saving rate rises mainly because automation raises the capital share, not because households become thriftier.
      • The ten-year real yield jumps from 5.1% to 13.6% on impact, pricing the growth boom.
      • Hand-to-mouth households lose two-fifths to two-thirds of consumption; keeping them on trend costs at most a fifth of GDP.
      The net fiscal impact of immigration should include gains from trade
      3d ago · ~1 min read
      A NBER paper finds native welfare rises in 14 of 17 OECD economies from skill-matched immigration; the author argues trade gains should be counted too.
      Key points
      • Battisti, Peri & Romano model immigration's labor-market and fiscal channels jointly, calibrated to 17 OECD countries over 2015-2019.
      • For inflows matching the skill composition of 2019-2024 arrivals, native welfare rises in 14 of 17 economies.
      • Lower-skilled natives gain mainly from higher pre-tax earnings; higher-skilled natives gain mainly from lower labor-tax burdens.
      • The author argues gains from trade should be counted, since they can be small yet overwhelm apparent net fiscal costs.
      • The author rejects the paper's claim that immigrants are equally productive but underpaid, favoring market size and increasing returns instead.
      The California billionaire tax is very likely a net revenue loser
      4d ago · ~1 min read
      Jaros and Rauh's NBER paper estimates California's one-time billionaire wealth tax raises about $30 billion, but lost income taxes from departures make net revenue negative.
      Key points
      • The one-time 5% tax on worldwide net worth above $1 billion is estimated to collect approximately $30 billion (range $28 to 36 billion), less than a third of Galle et al.'s $100 billion.
      • Publicly confirmed taxpayer migration has already eliminated nearly 30 percent of the Act's wealth tax base.
      • California's billionaires contribute $3.3–5.8 billion annually in state income taxes, and permanently lost collections from departures more than offset the one-time wealth tax.
      • Net present value is estimated at negative $38.9 billion, and 85 percent of 100,000 simulated revenue and discount rate combinations are negative.
      No, DataCenters are Not Crowding-Out Other Investments
      4d ago · ~3 min read
      Argues data center spending isn't really crowding out other investment; real rates rose mostly before the AI boom, and weak non-AI investment reflects tariffs and uncertainty.
      Key points
      • Loanable funds logic implies crowding out is less than one-for-one: $51b of data center spending crowds out less than $51b elsewhere.
      • Krugman's GDP-share chart misleads because GDP is rising; in dollars, real tech investment rose about 46% from 2023 Q2 to 2026 Q2, while other real investment rose about 3%.

      • Over a third of Western customers are open to new brands; Chinese exports to Italy more than doubled to 209,258 units and U.K. shipments rose about 86% to 365,672 in January-August.
      • Bain concludes manufacturers closing tech and brand gaps without pricing themselves out will gain most as the industry consolidates.

      How does the UK state pension compare with other countries?
      3h ago · ~4 min read
      UK basic state pension trails other wealthy nations, though private pensions narrow the gap; UK women face a bigger gender pension gap.
      Key points
      • After tax, UK retirement benefits are 54.2% of average UK earnings, versus an average of 63.2% across wealthy countries.
      • Including income from other occupational and personal pensions, which are more prevalent in the UK, narrows the gap.
      • UK spends a lower share of its economy on total retirement benefits than the average wealthy country, but chooses to spend more on items such as universal healthcare.
      • The typical gender pension gap in most wealthy countries is around 22.8% in favour of men; in the UK women typically get a pension a third less than men's (latest OECD UK data is for 2021).
      • Pensioner relative poverty is less likely than for families with children or working-age people, even after housing costs.

      The 'trillion pound' oil fund the government believes Britain could have built
      2d ago · ~3 min read
      The UK has created a £2bn Energy Wealth Fund from North Sea revenues, and Treasury modelling suggests a missed sovereign fund could have been worth about a trillion pounds, unlike Norway's $2.4tn fund.
      Key points
      • Chancellor John Healey announced the Energy Wealth Fund at the Jackdaw gas approval, with £2bn diverted from the National Wealth Fund.
      • Internal Treasury modelling assumed £200bn of historical North Sea receipts were invested, with no withdrawals, and grew at a Norway-comparable rate, giving about a trillion pounds.
      • Norway's fund, worth $2.4tn, draws down about 3% a year and must invest abroad; the UK fund can invest domestically in green energy.
      • UK energy tax revenues are forecast to dwindle as North Sea production slows, though Jackdaw and field extensions may ease the decline.
      • The author says nobody expects the £2bn fund to reach £2tn by 2056; the piece frames the trillion-pound figure as a 'what if' missed opportunity.
      The grads ditching the UK for a fresh start abroad
      2d ago · ~5 min read
      Young UK graduates are moving abroad for pay, lifestyle and opportunity, as graduate job competition hits a three-decade high.
      Key points
      • More than one in 10 UK final-year students plan to move abroad after graduating, up from 7.8% two years ago, per a survey of 15,000.
      • UK employers get 140 applications per graduate vacancy on average, up 14% from 2025 and from 30 twenty years ago (ISE).

      Audit regulator looking into Manchester City accounting
      3d ago · ~2 min read
      The audit regulator FRC is making inquiries into Manchester City's accounting but has not formally investigated; the club is appealing its Premier League breach verdict.
      Key points
      • The FRC took over "regulatory consideration" of the case from the Institute of Chartered Accountants in England and Wales on 30 September; no formal investigation has started.
      • An independent commission found City guilty of all charges over Premier League financial breaches from 2009-10 to 2017-18, including artificially inflating finances by more than £900m. The club is appealing.
      • The commission found City gave a "misleading impression" of the scheme used to disguise the origin of shareholder funds, and its auditors were "unaware" of it.
      • The FRC can examine auditor BDO and sanction accountants who are members of professional bodies, and has greater resources and powers than the existing process. BDO declined to comment.
      • Liam Byrne called for greater FRC powers to compel evidence and pursue directors, and asked the government to explain why reforms to close these gaps were abandoned.
      Trump sued over Canadian tariffs in latest legal challenge
      3d ago · ~3 min read
      Liberty Justice Center sues over 50% US levy on about $20bn of Canadian goods, arguing the 1930 Tariff Act's Section 338 doesn't authorise it.
      Key points
      • The White House imposed a 50% levy on some $20bn of Canadian goods after US-Canada trade talks broke down in August.
      • The Liberty Justice Center sued in the US Court of International Trade for Gellert Global Group, a New Jersey cheese business, seeking a ruling that the levies are unlawful and refunds with interest.
      • The suit argues Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930 was superseded by Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974, and that the White House did not establish a legal basis for the tariffs even if Section 338 applies.
      • It also says Section 338 requires a 30-day waiting period, but the cheese tariff was added just a week before collection began.
      • The group has filed for a preliminary injunction to block collection; it is its fourth case against Trump's tariffs, after its successful challenge to IEEPA tariffs struck down in February.

      Key points
      • Healey is reportedly minded not to increase CGT, partly over warnings it could cost money if wealthy people leave the country or defer selling assets.

      Nasdaq leads Wall Street gains as SpaceX, Super Micro boost AI sentiment
      2d ago · ~2 min read
      Nasdaq gained 1.28% as SpaceX and Super Micro results eased AI-spending worries; Netflix fell on a weak forecast, AT&T slid despite beating estimates.
      Key points
      • Nasdaq Composite rose 1.28% to 26,762.48 at 2:21 p.m. ET, with the S&P 500 up 0.73% and the Dow up 0.58%.
      • SpaceX shares rose 7% after its first profit as a public company; Musk called a SpaceX-Tesla merger "a good idea," lifting Tesla 3.5%.
      • Super Micro jumped more than 9% after forecasting quarterly revenue and profit margin above estimates, signaling resilient AI demand.
      • Netflix sank 4.5% on a weaker-than-expected fourth-quarter profit forecast; AT&T fell 6.3% despite beating third-quarter expectations.
      • Strategist Marc Dizard said the AI trade "is still intact," but warned sentiment can shift quickly ahead of Meta and Tesla earnings.
      Stocks rise on AI-led earnings, France’s 10-year bond yield hits 5%
      2d ago · ~3 min read
      Stock indexes rose on upbeat earnings while France's 10-year yield hit 5% for the first time since 2002 amid economic worries.
      Key points
      • Dow rose 0.15% to 52,572.65, S&P 500 rose 0.35% to 7,739.72, and Nasdaq Composite rose 0.86% to 26,651.94.
      • SpaceX shares rose about 10% after Elon Musk called a SpaceX-Tesla merger "a good idea"; SpaceX posted its first profit as a public company.
      • France's 10-year yield hit 5% for the first time since 2002 amid protests; Carrefour fell 13% after cutting guidance.
      • Super Micro shares jumped more than 9% after forecasting quarterly revenue and profit margin above Wall Street estimates.
      • NextDC shed nearly half its market capitalization after sharply cutting revenue forecasts and putting future developments on hold.
      Dollar dips as traders wait on Fed; Aussie gains
      2d ago · ~2 min read
      Dollar was mixed as traders awaited next week's Fed meeting amid oil-driven inflation worries; the Aussie rose on hawkish RBA comments.
      Key points
      • Dollar index fell 0.03% to 101.76; euro down 0.11% at $1.1224.
      • Oil jumped more than $2 after Iran said there was little reason to expect diplomatic progress with the US; Brent is near five-month highs.
      • Australian dollar rose 0.24% to $0.702 after RBA Deputy Governor Andrew Hauser said the inflation outlook looked "materially worse."
      • French 10-year borrowing costs hit 24-year highs amid protests and a worsening fiscal outlook.
      • Sterling was flat after UK inflation rose more than expected, strengthening the case for a Bank of England rate increase in November.

      ECB 6 items this week
      ECB Consumer Expectations Survey results – September 2026
      19h ago · ~4 min read
      ECB consumer survey: September 2026 perceived inflation rose to 3.6%, 12-month inflation expectations to 3.1%, and growth expectations fell to -1.5%.
      Key points
      • Perceived inflation over the past 12 months rose to 3.6% from 3.5%; expected inflation over the next 12 months rose to 3.1% from 3.0%.
      • Expected inflation three and five years ahead was unchanged at 2.9% and 2.5%.
      • Expected economic growth over the next 12 months fell to -1.5% from -1.2%; expected unemployment in 12 months rose to 11.3% from 11.0%.
      • Expected nominal income growth rose to 1.1% from 1.0%; expected spending growth rose to 3.7% from 3.6%.
      • Expected home price growth rose to 3.5% from 3.4%; expected mortgage rates were unchanged at 4.9%.
      Piero Cipollone: Pontes and Appia: unlocking opportunities for banks
      2d ago
      Philip R. Lane: Euro liquidity lines and the international monetary systemTitle partly delivers
      2d ago · ~6 min read
      ECB's Lane argues euro liquidity lines, including the enhanced EUREP repo facility and swaps, mitigate cross-border euro funding shocks and support the euro's global role.
      Key points
      • EUREP now offers standing access in principle to all central banks, excluding those barred on money laundering, terrorist financing or sanctions grounds, with loans at the MRO rate plus a spread.
      • Each central bank can borrow up to €50 billion per single transaction, with maturities from one day to one week; drawing becomes possible from Q4 2026.
      • More than thirty central banks expressed interest in EUREP in six months, and the first foreign onboardings were completed in early October.
      • Swap lines complement EUREP by working through the bank credit channel, not requiring euro collateral, and carrying stronger signalling effects, but they are limited to highly-rated backers.
      • Lane says liquidity lines also support the euro's international role, which strengthens ECB monetary policy transmission and reduces the sensitivity of euro area import prices to external shocks.
      Title check: Title suggests the wider international monetary system; text focuses on ECB euro liquidity lines and euro's global role.
      Frank Elderson: Interview with Die Presse
      3d ago · ~7 min read
      Elderson argues bank regulation costs are worth it, says Europe's main competitiveness problem is market fragmentation, and proportionality should not weaken core standards.
      Key points
      • The costs of the 2008 financial crisis far outweigh the cost of regulation; post-crisis reforms left banks well capitalised, liquid and more profitable.
      • Fragmentation is the biggest obstacle to European competitiveness; the fix is integration (banking union, European deposit insurance), not deregulation.
      • Proposes raising the small, non-complex bank threshold from €5 billion to €10 billion, bringing about 150 more banks under the simpler regime.
      • Opposes loosening Basel III: minimum international standards are needed, and loosening Pillar 1 could just shift the burden to supervision.
      • Banks must submit AI-related cyber risk action plans by end of October; private credit links between banks and non-banks could concentrate hidden risks.
      Frank Elderson: From climate and nature risks to economic resilience and competitiveness
      4d ago · ~19 min read
      Elderson argues climate and nature shocks are now macroeconomic risks; adaptation, electrification and predictable policy are needed for resilience and competitiveness.
      Key points
      • ECB research suggests severe water scarcity alone could put up to 24% of the euro area's economic output at risk.
      • Regional economic activity is estimated to be 1.5% lower two years after a heatwave and 3% lower four years after a drought.
      • Adaptation and mitigation are both needed; the more effectively we mitigate, the lower future adaptation costs will be.
      • Electricity is only around a quarter of final energy consumption in Europe, so electrification and renewables can cut fossil fuel dependence.
      • Policy uncertainty and financing constraints slow green investment, so Elderson calls for predictability, no backtracking on legislative commitments, and deeper capital markets.
      Christine Lagarde: IMFC Statement
      6d ago · ~6 min read
      Euro area growth is holding up, but energy-driven inflation stays well above target into 2027; a measured rate response remains appropriate.
      Key points
      • Headline inflation rose to 3.8% in September, mostly from energy; core inflation increased slightly to 2.5%.
      • The Governing Council raised key ECB rates by 25 basis points in September, following a same-size increase in June.
      • Lagarde sees no evidence of significant energy pass-through to other prices and wages beyond what is projected.
      • ECB staff projections foresee growth of 0.9% in 2026, 1.4% in 2027 and 1.5% in 2028, with downside risks.
      • The digital euro Regulation should be adopted by end-2026, with a 12-month internal pilot in the second half of 2027.
      Bank of England 2 items this week
      Regulators unveil plans to simplify Senior Manager and Certification Regime
      1d ago · ~4 min read
      UK regulators propose streamlining the Senior Managers and Certification Regime, removing pre-approval for some roles and the Certification Regime, to save firms about £2 billion over 10 years; consultations close 22 January.
      Key points
      • Regulator pre-approval removed for roles including Chief Actuaries and Chairs of Risk and Audit Committees; firms notify instead.
      • Certification Regime removed, affecting around 139,000 individuals below senior manager level.
      • Conduct rule breach reporting threshold raised for most staff below senior manager level.
      • CEOs, CFOs and Chairs keep regulator pre-approval; regulators can require it for other notified roles in high-risk cases.
      • Consultations open until 22 January, with policy expected in 2027; some reforms need legislation.
      Minutes of the Securities Lending Committee meeting – September 2026
      1d ago · ~2 min read
      Securities Lending Committee minutes: firms are broadly on track for T+1 in October 2027 but differ widely in readiness, with tax, tokenisation and AI also discussed.
      Key points
      • Industry is broadly on track for T+1 implementation in October 2027, but readiness differs significantly between firms.
      • Holding-period and record date rules across national tax regimes create operational challenges; the group called for greater tax harmonisation.
      • Remaining T+1 challenges include securities lending recalls, collateral sourcing, funding, partial settlements and agent lender capabilities.
      • A legal case could re-characterise a securities financing transaction as a secured loan, affecting repos, securities lending and contractual certainty.
      • AI use is mainly efficiency-driven, focused on administrative tasks and recall processing; concerns remain on governance, resilience, vendor oversight and data security.
      BoE speeches 3 items this week
      Physical shocks and economic resilience: a central bank perspective − speech by James Talbot
      18h ago · ~19 min read
      Physical climate shocks move output and inflation, and eroding insurance and fiscal buffers makes them more persistent, so central banks must factor them into policy.
      Key points
      • Annual global weather-related damages exceeded $200bn in 2025, more than double the early-2000s level.
      • Recent research estimates 30 days of low water on the Rhine cuts German industrial production by around 1%.
      • The October 2024 Valencia floods caused losses above 20% of regional GDP, but high insurance cover limited the hit to Spanish Q4 GDP growth (0.1 to 0.2 percentage points).
      • Bank of England staff modelling suggests UK uninsured mortgagors could rise from around 5% today to 7-10% in 2050, or up to 16% after a severe flood.
      • Insurance alone cannot close protection gaps; adaptation, risk prevention and better data are also needed.
      The PRA’s second decade − speech by Katharine Braddick
      1d ago · ~7 min read
      PRA head Katharine Braddick previews the regulator's first five-year strategy, built on resilience, growth, innovation and transparency, including mortgage model reforms.
      Key points
      • Next year the PRA will publish its first five-year strategy, built on four priorities: resilience, growth, innovation and transparency.
      • Resilience: a new PRA directorate for operational and cyber resilience, reporting directly to Braddick, and a joint FCA supervisory statement by the end of the year.
      • Growth: a framework for the secondary competitiveness and growth objective, and a consultation to index regulatory thresholds to nominal growth, a first among major financial regulators.
      • With the FCA, proposals to streamline the Senior Managers and Certification Regime, which the text says will halve its regulatory burden on firms while keeping clear accountability.
      • Transparency: a consultation in Spring next year on mortgage internal models, plus an independent review of hybrid mortgage model requirements.
      Learning about earnings: Can we rely on labour market slack to mitigate the latest supply shock? − speech by Megan Greene
      3d ago · ~37 min read
      Argues UK labour market slack is smaller than thought, raising wage-setting second-round inflation risk from the energy shock; wage growth likely plateaus near 3.5%.
      Key points
      • Private sector regular AWE pay growth was 3.0% in August, while the RTI median measure was 4.4% in September, an unusually large gap by historical standards.
      • Adjusted for compositional effects and bonuses, wage growth is 3.4% on AWE and 4.5% on RTI, both above levels consistent with the 2% target.
      • Forward-looking indicators (DMP expectations, pay settlements, Indeed Wage Tracker) point to wage growth levelling off near 3.5% rather than falling to 3%.
      • Unemployment fell from 5.2% to 4.7% and underlying employment growth is rising, so slack may have peaked.
      • Concludes the risk of second-round effects is greater than thought, so policy should proactively lean against them.
      On the Economy 1 item this week
      The Energy Assistance Landscape: Programs to Reduce Utility Cost Pressures
      1d ago · ~12 min read
      Federal LIHEAP and Weatherization programs are linked to fewer disconnections and better energy security, but reach is limited; Eighth District utilities offer varied bill discounts.
      Key points
      • LIHEAP reached 803,676 Eighth District households in 2025, or 17% of the 4.6 million who were eligible; Kentucky was highest at 21.2% and Mississippi lowest at 12.8%.
      • In one survey, 59% of LIHEAP recipients said their utility service would have been disconnected without the program.
      • DOE says weatherization yields $1.72 in household energy benefits and $2.78 in nonenergy benefits per dollar invested, but a randomized study found up-front costs were approximately double the actual energy savings.
      • Illinois has the most comprehensive set of utility discount programs in the Eighth District, with tiered discounts such as Ameren Illinois' natural gas program offering 5% to 84% off.
      • Utilities in other Eighth District states mostly offer lifeline discounts run through community action agencies; Duke Energy offers up to $300 in bill payment assistance.

      Tenth District Manufacturing Activity Increased Steadily in OctoberTitle partly delivers
      1d ago · ~3 min read
      Tenth District manufacturing activity rose in October, though the composite index eased from September, with firms reporting weaker orders and tight cost pass-through.
      Key points
      • Month-over-month composite index was 11 in October, down from 14 in September and up from 10 in August.

      The case for an Elastic Reserves system: Recommendations for the Federal Reserve’s balance sheet review
      4d ago · ~3 min read
      Chris Hughes proposes Elastic Reserves, letting banks' borrowing needs set reserve supply, to cut bank subsidies and the Fed's footprint while keeping rate control.
      Key points
      • The current Ample Reserves system pays banks more on reserves than they pay depositors, a subsidy to bank shareholders, and keeps Treasury and mortgage-backed prices higher than private investors would.
      • Elastic Reserves lets reserves expand when banks borrow and contract when they repay, with the Fed publishing lending terms and requiring advance collateral.
      • Three lending channels would support it: a redesigned Standing Repo Facility for overnight loans, a new Term Liquidity Facility with weekly four-month auctions, and the discount window for emergencies.
      • Liquidity rules would recognize pledged access to funds, and payment systems would offset transactions before settlement, reducing the reserves banks need.
      • An illustrative path runs from a balance sheet of roughly $6.7 trillion to around $4.5 trillion, with the final size depending on banks' actual needs.

      Surging Export Similarity is Fueling Advanced Economy Trade Tensions With China
